- List edges around M: M connects to E, L, and J.
- Any path from B to M must enter M via one of these three edges: (M, E), (M, L), or (M, J).

Check paths from B to these neighbors:
- To E: B–E is a direct edge.
- To L: B can reach L via B–E–L (triangle E-L-M).
- To J: B can reach J via B–I–J or B–P–J (both exist).

Therefore, to disconnect B from M we must cut all three edges incident to M, since cutting fewer leaves a remaining route through one of the other neighbors.

Verify minimality:
- Removing any two edges (e.g., cut (M,E) and (M,L)) still leaves B–I–J–M or B–P–J–M.
- Only by removing all three incident edges do all B–M paths vanish.

Final Answer:

Image name: FD-FR_task2_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 19
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
- Target nodes: H (black) and E (black).
- List neighbors and connections:

Around H:
- H connects to G and B, and also has an edge to I (a slanted line from H to I).
- So immediate routes from H toward E are via G, B, or I.

Toward E:
- E connects to F, L, M, and B (a line from E down to B).

Now enumerate H→E paths:
1) H–B–E (direct via B).
2) H–I–J–M–E (H–I; I–J; J–M; M–E).
3) H–I–P–J–M–E (alternative through P).
4) H–I–C–B–E (I–C; C–B; then to E).
5) H–B–C–I–J–M–E (looping but still uses either B or I on the H side).
6) H–G–F–E (H–G; G–F; F–E).

Observation:
- Every path from H to E must pass through at least one of the three nodes {B, I, F} immediately after leaving H’s neighborhood:
  - Via B: H–B–…–E
  - Via I: H–I–…–E
  - Via G then F: H–G–F–E (G connects only to H, F, X, K; to reach E it must go to F)
- Node G alone is not sufficient to cut because H has routes via B and I.
- Similarly, removing any single node among {B, I, F} leaves paths through the others. Therefore, a 1-node cut is impossible.

Check that removing {B, I} disconnects:
- H–B–… blocked (B removed).
- H–I–… blocked (I removed).
- H–G–F–E remains? H–G is present, G–F is present, but E is only reachable from F via F–E; since F remains, this path would still exist. So {B, I} is NOT sufficient.

Check pairs including F:
- Remove {F, B}:
  - H–G–F–E blocked at F.
  - H–B–E blocked at B.
  - Any path via I? H–I–… cannot reach E without using B, J–M, or other links to E. From I, routes go to J or P or C. From J to M to E; from P to J to M to E; from C to B to E. With B removed, the C→B→E route is gone, but I→J→M→E still exists unless we remove I or J or M. Since {F, B} leaves I, J, M intact, path H–I–J–M–E remains. So not enough.

- Remove {F, I}:
  - H–G–F–E blocked at F.
  - All I-based routes blocked at I.
  - Remaining possibility is via B: H–B–E is still open. So not enough.

- Remove {F, J}:
  - H–G–F–E blocked.
  - H–I–J–M–E and H–I–P–J–M–E blocked at J.
  - H–B–E still open. Not enough.

- Remove {F, M}:
  - H–G–F–E blocked.
  - All routes that go through J–M–E blocked at M.
  - But H–B–E remains. Not enough.

- Remove {F, B} was already shown insufficient; {F, C} or {F, P} also leave H–B–E.

Thus we need at least three nodes.

Try cut set {F, B, J}:
- Blocks G→F path.
- Blocks direct B route.
- Blocks I-based routes to E via J–M.
- Check for any remaining I-based alternative: I→C→B→E is blocked by B; I→P→… must go to J (P connects to J and O), and J is removed; no other edge reaches E.
- Any other way from H to E? From H only G, B, I. G route dead at F; B removed; I has no remaining path to E. Hence disconnected.

Is any pair sufficient? As argued, no pair works because:
- If F is not in the pair, path H–G–F–E exists.
- If B is not in the pair, path H–B–E exists.
- If J or M is not in the pair while removing F and B, the I→J→M→E path persists unless both I-path’s step to E is severed. Among downstream nodes, removing J alone suffices for I-routes after removing F and B. But with only two nodes, we must remove F and B or F and J or B and J; each leaves another route open as shown.

Therefore, minimum is 3. One valid minimal cut is {F, B, J}. Other equivalent triples include {F, B, M} or {F, B, I}.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [F, B, J]
--- Ground Truth ---

   - Therefore, to block the Y branch without touching P, we must remove Y or O.

2) Through B:
   - N–B–(right via H/G/K/X/F/E…)–…–I–C–N is a loop, but to reach P:
     - N–B–I–P (via I–P is not direct; I connects to C and to the long outer frame to J, but P is only via J or O. However, there is an edge I–P directly? No. I connects to C and to the bottom horizontal to J; P sits on that bottom line between J and O with a vertical up to O. Thus B branch can reach P by:
       - N–B–I–J–P, or
       - N–B–I–(bottom line)–…–J–P, or
       - N–B–I–(up C–O)–P.
   - Hence to block the B branch we must remove B, I, J, or O.

3) Through C:
   - N–C–O–P (short), or
   - N–C–I–J–P.
   - Thus blocking C branch requires removing C, O, I, or J.

Observe articulation:
- Node O lies on both Y→P and C→P (and also B→I→C→O→P route). If we remove O, paths that reach P via O disappear, but paths via J (e.g., N–B–I–J–P or N–C–I–J–P) remain.
- Node J blocks only the J-entry; paths via O (e.g., N–Y–O–P or N–C–O–P) remain.
- Therefore any cut must hit both entrances to P: O and J or earlier nodes that funnel all three N-branches before splitting.

Check minimal cut size:
- Can a single node remove all routes? Candidates:
  - Remove I: Paths N–Y–O–P and N–C–O–P still work. So no.
  - Remove B: Paths via Y and C still exist to O–P and J–P via I (through C–I). So no.
  - Remove C: Path N–Y–O–P and N–B–I–J–P remain. So no.
  - Remove Y: N–C–O–P and N–B–I–J–P remain. So no.
  - Remove O: N–B–I–J–P and possibly N–C–I–J–P remain. So no.
  - Remove J: N–Y–O–P and N–C–O–P remain. So no.
Hence at least 2 nodes are needed.

Find a 2-node cut:
- If we remove O and J, then P has no remaining neighbor, so all paths are cut. This works.
- Can any other pair of size 2 work? Yes, any pair that hits both “to-P entrances”: one must block O-side and the other block J-side. Examples: [O, I] doesn’t work because N–Y–O–P would be gone but J remains; yet without I, N could still reach J via N–Y–O… actually O is removed, so to J: N–B–I path is broken at I; but N–C–(no I path to J). However N can go to J via the outer rectangle from B? J connects along the bottom frame to the right to H and up outer frame to M to E to B etc., but there is a bottom outer edge from J to H? No, bottom outer edge goes from J right to H along the bottom? The image shows a large outer rectangle from J along bottom to H, up to E/M side? Yes: there is a bottom horizontal from J to H. Then N–B–H–(outer up/right/left)–… to J? H connects left to B and up to G/K loop, and down to the bottom line to J. So N–B–H–J would reach J even without I. Thus removing I plus O would still allow N–B–H–J–P. Therefore the reliable minimal cut is specifically removing both O and J.

Verify necessity at size 2:
- Any size-1 cut fails as shown.
- With size 2, removing [O, J] disconnects because P’s only neighbors are O and J. After removing both, P is isolated, making any N–P path impossible.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [O, J]
--- Ground Truth ---

Step-by-step reasoning:
- The two black nodes are N and P.
- List neighbors:
  - N connects to C, B, O, Y.
  - P connects to E, I, J, and O (via the diagonal line from P to O).
- Any N–P path must enter the neighborhood of P through one of {E, I, J, O}. Thus, a vertex cut must include at least one node from this set for every route from N.

Check possible routes from N to each of these four:
- To O: N–O is a direct edge, and O–P is a direct edge. So if O remains, N–P exists.
- To B/C: N connects to B and C; from there to I then to P; also from B straight down to E then to P; from B via edges to F through I then P etc. Hence, if I or E or J remains along with corresponding links, paths persist.
- To J: N→B→E→M→J or N→B→I→J, etc.
- To E: N→B→E (direct) then E→P.

Therefore, to block all paths, we must remove all four P-neighbors {O, I, E, J} or otherwise separate N from each of them. But there is also the direct N–O–P path, meaning O must be in any cut unless we instead disconnect N from O and simultaneously block all remaining routes via I, E, J. However, N connects to O directly; removing some other node cannot break that edge. Thus O must be removed.

After removing O, remaining possible connections from N to P are via:
- N→B→E→P
- N→B→I→P
- N→C→I→P
- N→B→I→J→P (but J is neighbor of P)
- N→B→E→(L/M triangle)→J→P, etc.

These all funnel through {E, I, J}. To eliminate all, we must remove E and either I or J? From B to J without I or E seems impossible: paths from B go to C, I, E, O, and N; with I and E blocked and O removed, only C and N remain. From C you can go to H and B; from H to G and I; with I removed, H cannot reach J either. So B cannot reach J if both E and I are removed. Thus removing {O, E, I} suffices.

Is a smaller cut possible (size 2)?
- Because of the direct N–O–P path, any size-2 cut that does not include O fails. If it includes O, we still must block both remaining disjoint routes via E and via I/J. With O removed, there are at least two internally disjoint paths from N to P: N–B–E–P and N–C–I–P (also N–B–I–P). To break both, we need at least two more nodes unless one node lies on all remaining paths. No single node does: E is on some, I on others, J on some via I or via E–M–J. Therefore, size 2 is impossible.

Verify minimal 3-node cut:
- Remove O: eliminates N–O–P.
- Remove E: breaks all paths using E (N–B–E–P and routes through E→M/L→J→P).
- Remove I: breaks all paths using I (N–C–I–P, N–B–I–P, and any route to J via I).
- After removing O, E, I, J has no connection from N’s side (since from N one must go through B or C; without E and I, there is no path from B/C/H/G side to J). Hence all N–P paths are severed.

Conclusion: Minimum is 3 with cut set {O, E, I} (other equivalent sets include {O, E, J} if we additionally ensure I cannot reach P? But I connects directly to P, so J alone doesn’t cover I. Thus {O, E, I} is a valid minimal set).

Final Answer:

1) Via M: E–M–J is a direct 2-edge path.
2) Via I/P: E–B–C–I–J or E–B–C–O–P–J or E–B–H–I–J, etc. All these go through either edge (I, J) or (P, J) as the last step to J.
3) Via D: paths like E–B–C–O–P–D–J or E–B–C–I–P–D–J, which require edge (D, J) at the end.

- To disconnect E from J, we must cut all ways into J that are reachable from E: through M, through I, through P, and through D. However, I and P routes both terminate at J via edges (I, J) and/or (P, J). If we remove both (I, J) and (P, J), any path through I or P is blocked. We must also block the M and D entries: remove (M, J) and (D, J).

- Verify minimality:
  • If any one of the four edges ((M, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J)) is kept, there exists a path from E reaching that neighbor (E→M; E→…→I; E→…→P; E→…→D respectively), so E–J would remain connected.
  • No smaller set suffices because J has exactly four disjoint incident edges that are all reachable from E; at least one cut per reachable incident edge of J is necessary.

Therefore the minimum cut is to remove all edges incident to J that are reachable from E: (M, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J).

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J)]

6) N–Y–C–H–... (then as in 4/5)
7) N–O–P–D–J–M–... (these upper routes eventually rejoin the middle band at B/C via the long outer edges; however, every descent to the lower area still must pass through either edge (C, H) or enter F’s local hub and then go through one of F–G/F–K/F–X to reach G)

Key observation:
- Any path from N to G must enter G through one of G’s three incident edges: (G, H), (G, K), or (G, X).
- Cutting all three of these edges isolates G from the rest of the graph, regardless of which route from N is taken (via B/F, via C/H, or via other detours reaching F or H).

Minimality:
- Removing only one or two of { (G, H), (G, K), (G, X) } leaves at least one remaining edge to G, and there are corresponding paths from N to that neighbor:
  - If (G, H) remains: N–C–H–G.
  - If (G, K) remains: N–B–F–K–G.
  - If (G, X) remains: N–B–F–X–G.
- Therefore, all three must be removed; no smaller cut disconnects N and G.

Final Answer:
